| **[ContactBench 1.0: A Benchmark for Everyday Contact-Rich Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24512v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contact-rich manipulation is essential to everyday activities, yet evaluating robots' ability to perform these activities remains challenging because of their diverse contact behaviors and the difficulty of reproducing tasks across laboratories. Drawing on observations of everyday human manipulation, we identify eight recurring skill categories that provide a practical framework for studying these capabilities. We introduce ContactBench 1.0, an extensible benchmark that instantiates these skills through 36 reference tasks and more than 40 object assets. The assets capture task-relevant geometry and contact behavior, support reproducible fabrication using consumer-grade 3D printers, and are paired with simulation models with tunable resistance loads. Controlled task variations enable evaluation of policy generalization. We evaluate recent vision-based and vision-tactile policy learning methods on six benchmark tasks. The results reveal that the evaluated vision-only policies struggle to perceive or react to contact events, while the evaluated visual-tactile policies do not meaningfully improve performance. By providing shared tasks and physical and simulated assets, ContactBench 1.0 establishes a foundation for reproducible evaluation of contact-rich manipulation research. The benchmark is open-sourced at https://jingyi-xiang.github.io/contactbench</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://jingyi-xiang.github.io/contactbench</p></details> |
| **[Learning to Compose: Recursive Self-Improvement of Shared Executable Stacks for Zero-Shot Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24464v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Individually competent robot skills can fail when composed: tools may lack a required capability, completion signals may misrepresent physical progress, and one skill may leave a state that violates its successor's prerequisites. We introduce Recursia, a recursive self-improvement framework that treats the shared executable stack, rather than individual task programs, as the persistent unit of improvement. The stack integrates tools, skills with execution contracts, handoffs, and verifiers. Source-side execution traces guide failure attribution and the generation of missing tools or repairs to existing shared components. Generality checks, mechanism tests, and paired selection and regression gates determine which changes become the next parent. The resulting stack is frozen and reused through declarative task adapters, allowing source-derived changes to support new compositions without target-side code repair. Experiments cover LIBERO-Pro Long simulation and a physical Franka FR3 equipped with a Franka Hand. In simulation, Recursia improves success over ASPIRE by 5.8 percentage points under Pos perturbations and 15.1 points under Task perturbations. Across three physical compositional target tasks, it improves mean success over the Skill Document Only baseline by 40 percentage points. Analyses of constituent execution, candidate rejection, and inference drivers distinguish local mechanism improvements from complete task success and characterize the cost of feedback-conditioned orchestration. These results examine shared executable interfaces as a concrete mechanism for compositional transfer in robot programming.</p></details> |  |
| **[Tension Tells the Tail: Cable-Obstacle Interaction Sensing for Tethered Mobile Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24449v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tethered mobile robots can sustain long-duration operation through continuous power supply and stable communication via their cables. However, cable-environment interaction causes unintended obstacle displacement, cable snagging or entanglement, and even cable damage, leading to mission failure. To assess the risk of individual cable-obstacle contacts, this study investigates cable tension as a sensing modality for estimating whether a contacted obstacle is movable or non-movable. We conducted experiments with a tethered mobile robot and collected tension data while varying robot velocity, contact angle between the obstacle and cable, obstacle mass, and obstacle side-surface material. The results show that obstacle movability is characterized by the initial tension rise and, more importantly, by whether the tension relaxes or remains high after its peak. Based on these observations, we select six features from the tension profiles and train a logistic-regression classifier using 117 labeled trials. The proposed method distinguishes movable and non-movable cases with a balanced accuracy of 95.5% after 30 cm of post-contact travel. These results demonstrate the potential of cable tension for risk assessment of cable-obstacle interactions and provide a step toward online, cable-aware autonomous navigation.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 11 figures. Accepted to 2026 IEEE International Symposium on Safety, Security, and Rescue Robotics (SSRR)</p></details> |
| **[RoboEdit: Turning Human Manipulation Videos into Scalable Robot Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18948v4)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Collecting robot hand-object interaction data is costly and embodiment-specific, yet abundant human-object videos remain unusable for robot training. We present RoboEdit, a human-to-robot video editing suite that transforms human manipulation videos into action-consistent, physically plausible robot videos with aligned 3D hand states. To enable scalable supervision, we introduce RoboEdit-ADC, an automatic pipeline that reconstructs and retargets 3D interactions from RGB videos across embodiments. This pipeline generates RoboEdit-14M, a large-scale dataset of 174K aligned video pairs (14M frames) spanning seven robot embodiments, diverse scenes, and interaction types. The core editing engine, RoboEdit-Trans, employs cross-embodiment adaptation modules to preserve temporal coherence while adapting appearance and motion. It further integrates a 3D Robot-State Decoder to recover per-frame hand states for structured motion supervision. Experiments show that RoboEdit achieves state-of-the-art editing quality and supports downstream robot control policies in real-world manipulation tasks. Ultimately, the RoboEdit suite unlocks the vast potential of unlabeled human videos, providing scalable, high-fidelity visual and 3D motion supervision for generalizable robot learning. Project webpage: https://roboedit.github.io/</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 14 figures, 7 tables, including supplementary material. Revised version with additional experiments and implementation details</p></details> |
| **[RARA: Robust Policy Fine-Tuning through Retrieval-Anchored Representation Alignment for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13578v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pre-trained robot policies, such as Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, provide a strong foundation for generalist robotic manipulation, but adapting them to new tasks with only a few demonstrations remains challenging. Directly fine-tuning a pre-trained policy often achieves strong in-distribution performance, yet it can distort the representations learned during pre-training and weaken the model's generalization ability under out-of-distribution shifts. We propose Retrieval-Anchored Representation Alignment (RARA), a two-stage adaptation method, to address this issue. First, RARA retrieves representation features from pretraining with similar task intent and motion, and aligns the policy's representations of the target demonstrations with the pre-trained representations of the retrieved demonstrations. Second, RARA fine-tunes the policy on the target demonstrations while limiting how much its representations of the retrieved demonstrations change during fine-tuning. By reducing the representation mismatch before action learning and preserving pre-trained knowledge during adaptation, RARA limits unnecessary representation drift during fine-tuning. Experiments with Diffusion Policy and GR00T on 13 tasks in RoboTwin, RoboCasa, and on a real robot show that RARA improves the average success of fine-tuning by 9.5 points in-distribution and 13.6 points out-of-distribution, and outperforms all compared baselines. Project page: https://shua-kang.github.io/RARA_robot/</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages, 11 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[LynxScenes: A Large-Scale Real-World Benchmark for Outdoor Wheel-Legged Robot Perception, Navigation, Imagination and Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18093v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wheel-legged robots operating in outdoor spaces shared with humans require accurate perception, socially compliant navigation, future-scene reasoning, and scene reconstruction. However, existing datasets typically support these capabilities in isolation and rarely provide dense spatial and behavior-level supervision on real wheel-legged platforms. We introduce LynxScenes, a large-scale real-world dataset and benchmark collected with a LYNX M20 wheel-legged robot. It contains approximately 100K 30-s navigation clips spanning 833.6 h and 3,294.7 km, with 3.0M annotated frames. The synchronized recordings include four fisheye cameras, dual LiDARs, IMU, odometry, and robot states. Annotations comprise 20.82M oriented 3D boxes across 27 categories, 13.89M road-structure annotations, and 20 navigation instructions organized into three layers and aligned with continuous future trajectories. LynxScenes supports four complementary tasks: instruction-conditioned trajectory prediction, BEV-based perception of 3D objects and road structures, future scene prediction, and multi-view scene reconstruction. For trajectory prediction, we further introduce a log-replay-based pseudo-closed-loop evaluator that measures safety, progress, and instruction compliance beyond trajectory imitation. Extensive evaluations across representative navigation, perception, world-model, and reconstruction methods reveal substantial remaining challenges in close-range outdoor robot navigation. The dataset and evaluation toolkit are planned for public release.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 9 figures. Includes appendices</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Human-Derived Motion: Exploring Robot-Specific Execution for Construction Humanoids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24312v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Construction productivity has remained a per- sistent challenge, motivating growing interest in humanoid robots that can operate in workspaces and with tools designed for human workers. Realizing this potential requires effective motion control, for which human-derived motion transfer has emerged as a promising approach by capturing and retargeting human demonstrations to humanoid robots. While promising, however, human-derived motion transfer may not fully define how a humanoid should ultimately execute the transferred motion. Once task-relevant motion information has been con- veyed, the humanoid may also exploit its own embodiment through motions that depart from the human demonstration. This study examines whether such Robot-Specific Motion offers an advantage in motion execution efficiency over Human- Derived Motion. On an adult-sized Unitree H1-2, Human- Derived Motion was generated through vision-based capture and retargeting, whereas Robot-Specific Motion used inverse kinematics and minimum-jerk transfers to reach the same targets while departing from the demonstrated posture, as verified using Rapid Upper Limb Assessment (RULA). Across a pick-and-stack sequence representing construction material handling, with four matched motion targets and three trials per condition, Robot-Specific Motion achieved 12.87 s versus 25.03 s in execution time, 0.92 m versus 4.33 m in trajectory length, and 58.7 J versus 247.3 J in two-arm mechanical work. These findings suggest that allowing humanoids to depart from demonstrated human motion may offer additional opportunities for more efficient motion execution, while broader construction tasks are needed to determine whether such differences trans- late into productivity gains.</p></details> |  |
| **[How exposed is work to robotics? Occupational, industry, and geographic measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24267v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exposure indices that map technical capabilities onto occupational abilities have become a standard tool for anticipating how AI may reshape work, but existing measures either cover software AI or proxy robotics through patents and installation data that reflect the industrial robots of past decades. We construct the first abilities-based exposure measures for robotics and physical AI, and the first to span occupations, industries, and geographies. Following the framework of Felten et al. (2018, 2021), we build a robotics capability taxonomy of 16 capabilities in eight domains from 78 academic, industry, and standards sources, use a large language model to score the relatedness of each capability to the 52 ONET work abilities, and aggregate to Robotics Occupational Exposure (ROE) for 774 occupations, Robotics Industry Exposure (RIE) for 250 NAICS industries, and county-level Robotics Geographic Exposure (RGE). We find that robotics exposure concentrates in physically intensive work such as construction, farming, transportation, and production, and in industries such as logging, mining, trucking, and warehousing. Unlike generative-AI exposure, ROE is higher in occupations with lower wages, lower education requirements, and less creative-ability content. Physical collaboration and assistance, terrain-based locomotion, grasping and handling, and task and functional reasoning move the exposure ranking most, yet no single capability carries the index. Because the measures capture technical relatedness rather than readiness, cost, or adoption, they should be read as an upper bound on where physical automation could affect work.</p></details> |  |
| **[Flow structure decides whether a robot searching for odour should follow the wind](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20970v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robots searching for odour usually surge upwind on contact, steering by the wind sensed now. In simulated reversing wind this rule collapses, favouring controllers that hold an earlier heading. We show that on our benchmark the collapse came from the simulated anemometer. Reading the nominal wind, wind-following controllers reached the source in at most 3.5% of episodes. Reading the odour-carrying flow, the best reached 90%, ahead of a held heading. The winner then depends on the flow. A held heading won in smooth fields that reverse quickly and lost in a stochastic field. A controller-free measure of the sensed wind ranked a moth-inspired follower's success. On a quadruped in a reversing fan arena, wind-following controllers were pushed away from the source, while a heading held from a source-facing start was not. Benchmarks should state which wind their sensor reads and test more than one flow structure.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Reinforcement Learning for Space Robotics: Fast Training and Reliable Real-World Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24241v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This work demonstrates the use of a fast simulation-based training framework to learn control policies using reinforcement learning. The hardware requirements are minimal and the simulation-learned policies transfer reliably to the real world. With this setup, we show that a quadruped robot can learn to traverse diverse real-world terrains reliably---including in a lunar-analog facility---after less than 20 minutes of training on a single consumer-grade GPU. These results provide a glimpse of a future where reinforcement learning is a crucial tool in developing more capable and robust space robotic systems. Video demonstrations are available at https://youtu.be/vUFJiZdJaQ0.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prese...</summary><p>Presented at the Space Robotics Workshop at IROS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Rollbot 2: Agile Locomotion by a Single-Actuator Spherical Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24129v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reducing actuator count simplifies robot hardware but also removes independent inputs for maneuvering. The original Rollbot steered by modulating a quasi-stable helical orbit, but moved slowly and relied on external motion capture. Rollbot 2 preserves the single-actuator design while its redesigned mass distribution enables near-straight rolling and more direct travel toward commanded paths and beacon targets. Onboard inertial and ultra-wideband sensing support learned steering, beacon seeking, and waypoint following through one motor-velocity command. Rollbot 2 autonomously approaches UWB beacons without motion capture and completes a 468~m manually commanded indoor route at 0.87~m/s. On the shared 4.8~m ``N'' waypoint benchmark, all 16 trials complete the path with a mean traversal time of 10.8~s and mean time-averaged deviation of 7~cm. Its fastest traversal takes 8.0~s, over 70 times faster than the original Rollbot's 564~s run despite minor protocol differences. These results show how mechanical redesign, onboard sensing, and learning-based control improve the agility and autonomy of a single-actuator spherical robot.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning Robot Metareasoning: Adaptive Computation Budgets for Real-Time Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23662v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Planning while moving faces an historical trade-off between deliberation and reactivity. A planner running concurrently with execution needs time to compute, so the robot must commit to an open-loop action chunk for that duration: a longer commitment buys a better plan at the cost of feedback frequency. Existing controllers fix this action commitment, and consequently the computation budget, before deployment. However, the optimal computation time is highly state-dependent (due to factors such as unmodeled dynamics, safety constraints, and marginal optimisation gains), i.e. dynamic, therefore offline static tuning risks leaving systems blind during critical maneuvers or unable to find global effective solutions. We cast the identification of the optimal computation budget as a semi-Markov decision process (SMDP) in which a single decision defines both the robot action commitments and the time allocated to the solver. To solve this, we train a reinforcement-learning policy conditioned on the current state, the executing plan, and inexpensive planner statistics. Across ground-robot navigation, agile quadrotor flight, and contact-rich manipulation, the learned policy changes its budget with the demands of the scenario, discovering efficient reasoning patterns that outperform fixed-budget baselines. https://lorenzomagnino.github.io/learning-robot-metareasoning-review/</p></details> |  |
| **[EDAR: Learning Environment-Dependent Action Representations for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11427v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning effective action representations is critical for robotic manipulation, where raw control trajectories are often noisy, redundant, and difficult to model directly. Existing methods mainly encode the structure of the action stream itself, treating the role of actions in the environment as implicit. Yet manipulation is about changing the world: the same action segment can induce different outcomes under different scene contexts, making action semantics inherently environment-dependent. We propose EDAR, an Environment-Dependent Action Representation that grounds action tokens in both executable control structure and expected visual consequences. By coupling motor commands with their environment-conditioned effects, EDAR encourages the learned action space to capture interaction semantics rather than merely command-level patterns. Experiments on simulated and real-robot manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that EDAR improves downstream policy learning, especially in long-horizon manipulation. These results highlight the importance of grounding action representations in executable control structure and environment-conditioned visual change.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Tendon-Driven Robotic Jellyfish with Constrained Soft Actuation and Depth Control via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29132v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Jellyfish-inspired robots offer a compliant and efficient approach to underwater locomotion, but achieving large deformation together with repeatable actuation and closed-loop control remains challenging. In this work, we present a tendon-driven robotic jellyfish with constrained soft actuation. Each actuator combines a flexible substrate with discrete constraints, enabling bending up to \(150^\circ\) with an approximately linear tendon displacement-bending relationship. Eight actuators driven by four servos allow the robot to perform stable swimming, attitude adjustment, and self-righting. Based on the linear actuation, a reinforcement-learning controller is further developed, enabling closed-loop depth regulation in both simulation and physical experiments. These results show that mechanical constraints can improve the controllability of soft actuation while preserving compliant jellyfish-like motion, providing a route toward manoeuvrable and autonomous jellyfish robots.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 8 figures. Corrected a typo in an author name in the arXiv metadata. No changes to the manuscript</p></details> |
| **[AWAM: Active Trajectory-Conditioned View Imagination for Robust Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23396v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rapid progress in embodied intelligence has enabled increasingly capable robots to follow language instructions and manipulate diverse objects. However, strong results in structured environments do not automatically transfer to open-world conditions, where clutter, occlusion, and changing viewpoints can obscure the spatial relationships required for action. To address this perceptual gap, a virtual active-perception paradigm is introduced. AWAM, an Active World-Action Model actively plans where to observe and imagines what will be seen along the planned trajectory before acting. Task-related virtual camera motion is predicted and used as a structured query into a geometry-conditioned world model, producing a reusable spatial prior for manipulation, which is later compressed into a fixed memory and fused with live observations through cross-attention for diffusion-based action prediction. AWAM achieves competitive success rates with 75.9% on LIBERO-Occ, 62.4% on LIBERO-Plus, 76.5% on RLBench-OG Occlusion1, and 94.4% in real-robot trials. These results support improved robustness to the evaluated occlusions and distribution shifts, addressing part of the gap between structured manipulation and less controlled environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Enabling Tidying Tasks in Robotic Vacuum Cleaners via a Geometry-Induced Self-Locking Deployable Linkage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20150v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic vacuum cleaners must extend their functionality within tight limits on size, cost, and durability. Deployable linkages with few actuators meet these limits, but their backdrivability makes it difficult to hold a load-bearing posture without continuous motor torque. We introduce a two-stage deployable linkage that locks through its own geometry. At full deployment, an internal three-link subchain approaches a collinear, near-singular configuration in which the input-output transmission ratio tends to zero; plate loads therefore leave only a small residual back-driving torque, which drive-train friction and a lightweight magnetic detent absorb. A single actuator performs deployment and locking, a passive chassis-mounted trigger releases the lock for retraction, and a second actuator adjusts only the deployment height. We integrate the mechanism into a commercial robotic vacuum cleaner and validate it through a 100-cycle repeatability test, a quasi-static load test in which the locked plate held 50 N with the motor de-energized, and 140 pushing-type tidying trials on seven household objects against a commercial arm-equipped vacuum robot. We derive the analytical holding condition and discuss its limits as an intermediate design point between passive compliance and articulated manipulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 6 figures, 1 table. v2: corrected author name, added minor revisions</p></details> |
| **[Analytic Robot Self-Model: Learning Screw-Axis Kinematics for Rendering and Model-Based Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15558v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual self-modelling lets robots learn body models from observations, but such models usually encode kinematics implicitly rather than exposing metric joint parameters for computation and control. Moreover, a good fit to images does not by itself establish accurate kinematics. Here we present an explicit analytic robot self-model that requires neither deformation networks nor URDF/CAD templates. Multi-view images and joint readings establish Gaussian appearance and provide motion evidence for rigid link assignments, whereas sparse end-effector poses independently identify metric screw axes. The resulting closed-form forward kinematics and analytic Jacobian can be evaluated beyond the fitting ranges. We validate the model on a simulated seven-axis arm, a simulated twelve-joint quadruped and a real six-axis arm. In simulation, it achieves millimetre-level reaching and pick-and-place, as well as closed-loop quadruped walking. On the real arm, it renders held-out configurations, predicts end-effector positions with a median error of 6.4 mm, compared with 11.7 mm for a calibrated nominal model, and supports reaching with external marker feedback. A controlled comparison shows that image fit alone is not enough: two Panda models differing only in their joint axes are 0.55 dB apart in PSNR, yet their end-effector prediction errors differ 13.7-fold.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 6 figures, supplementary information included</p></details> |
| **[From Sliding Windows to Self-evolving Lifelong Memory: A Dual-perspective Survey of Memory Paradigms for Long-Horizon Robotics VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23319v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon robotic tasks expose a fundamental limitation of current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models because their restricted temporal context pre-vents effective accumulation and reuse of past experiences. As a result, memory has become a fundamental component for enabling embodied agents to perform long-horizon reasoning and adaptive decision-making. This survey presents a systematic taxonomy of VLA memory mechanisms based on memory scope and persistence, categorizing existing approaches into five representative paradigms and one emerging frontier. Based on this taxonomy, we compare representative VLA memory systems in terms of memory characteristics, architectural com-plexity, and open-source maturity, revealing a clear trade-off in which increasing architectural complexity is generally accompanied by decreasing open-source ma-turity. Finally, we discuss the key challenges in building full-lifecycle memory systems, including efficient memory management and lifelong knowledge evolu-tion. Overall, this survey provides a unified perspective on memory architectures for VLA agents and offers insights into the development of lifelong embodied in-telligence.</p></details> | 10 pages, 1 figure |
